metrics = {'T_median': 24.075, 'T_median_ok': False, 'T_median_ratio': 0.12190259185417261, 'contaminated_files': {}, ...}

    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Check the converted dataset matches the reference in size, variables and ranges.
    
        Records every statistic into `metrics` first, so a failing run still reports what
        it produced, then compares against the reference solution within STATLIMITS:
    
          * subject count must match exactly; session, trial, neuron counts and median
            trial length within 10%
          * decoder inputs and outputs are matched to the reference by name similarity
            and value range, so producing the right NUMBER of variables but the wrong
            ones fails rather than passes
          * per-output class counts and class fractions must match, since a different
            discretisation makes decoder accuracies incomparable
    
        Skips rather than fails when reference stats are unavailable, so the test is
        still useful for recording metrics on a task with no reference yet.
        """
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi))+1
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
            # Verdict recorded where the threshold is applied; the assert below reads it back.
            # The assert loop bails on its first failure, so computing these here is what makes
            # a later field's result knowable after an earlier one has failed.
            metrics[field+'_ok'] = bool(
                ratio_within_limits(metrics[field+'_ratio'], STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']))
    
        # Seed a per-variable verdict for every REFERENCE output, so a variable the agent never
        # produced -- and which therefore never appears in output_matches below -- counts as a
        # miss rather than shrinking the denominator of the fraction categories.
        reference_output_names = list(reference_data_stats['data_summary'].get('output_range') or [])
        for ref_name in reference_output_names:
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated
        # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any
        # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>
        # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)
            if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0
            else:
                scale = max(scale, 1e-6)
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))
        mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0
        metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error
        # Recorded ONLY when there is something to match. A task with no decoder inputs
        # (allen2p: dinput == 0, input_range == {}) has no input ranges to compare, so leaving
        # the key unset scores the category undefined and drops it from the mean -- rather than
        # awarding a free 1.0 for a check that never happened. Matches undefined_categories()
        # in lesion_analysis.py, which singles out this same case. The assert below is guarded
        # by the same condition, so it never reads a key that was not written.
        if len(input_matches):
            metrics['input_range_ok'] = bool(
                mean_input_range_error <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'])
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]
    
        # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.
        mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
        metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(abs(sub_hi - ref_hi))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi) + 1)
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi) + 1)
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())
            if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None
    
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'])
            # A None error means the class counts differ, so there is no comparable fraction
            # vector. That scores as a miss, but is NOT asserted on below -- the nclasses check
            # already fails that case, and asserting here too would change what the suite
            # rejects. Verdict and assert diverge only here, deliberately.
            frac_error = metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}']
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                frac_error is not None and frac_error <= STATLIMITS['output_fraction_error'])
    
        for field in fields_check:
>           assert metrics[field+'_ok'], f"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})"

list of sessions\n    62→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    63→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    64→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    65→            ...\n    66→        ],\n    67→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    68→            ...\n    69→        ],\n    70→        ...\n    71→    ],\n    72→\n    73→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    74→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    75→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    76→            ...\n    77→        ],\n    78→        ...\n    79→    ],\n    80→\n    81→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    82→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    83→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    84→            ...\n    85→        ],\n    86→        ...\n    87→    ],\n    88→\n    89→    'subjects': list of str, # list of sessions\n    94→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    95→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    96→\n    97→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    98→    'output_names': list of str, # description of output tasks\n   106→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   107→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   108→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   109→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   110→        ...\n   111→    }\n   112→}\n   113→```\n   114→\n   115→### Key Format Requirements\n   116→\n   117→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   118→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   119→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   120→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   121→\n   122→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   123→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   124→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   125→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   126→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   127→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   128→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   129→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   130→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   131→\n   132→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   133→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   134→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   135→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   136→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   137→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   138→\n   139→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   140→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   141→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   142→\n   143→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   144→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   145→\n   146→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   147→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   148→\n   149→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   150→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   151→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   152→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   153→\n   154→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   155→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   156→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   157→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   158→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   159→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   160→\n   161→## Decoder Reference\n   162→\n   163→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   164→- Optional arguments:\n   165→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   166→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   167→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   168→\n   169→### Purpose\n   170→\n   171→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   172→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   173→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   174→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   175→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   176→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   177→\n   178→### Consistency requirements\n   179→\n   180→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   181→- Loading of data\n   182→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   183→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   184→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   185→\n   186→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. Discretized into bins:\n  - 0: < 2 cm/s\n  - 1: 2-10 cm/s\n  - 2: 10-20 cm/s\n  - 3: 20-40 cm/s\n  - 4: > 40 cm/s\n- Lick, time-varying. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n- Reward zone location, per-trial. 0 = A, 1 = B, 2 = C\n- Reward outcome, per-trial. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. All required files must be created:\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n  - Decoder accuracy\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. The NWB's `Deconvolved` array is instead suite2p's own deconvolution of raw fluorescence.\n   116→\n   117→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed? \n   118→\n   119→i. dF/F is computed from F and Fneu and then deconvolved into \"events\", by a copy of the paper's own `preprocessing.dff`. Per plane: restrict to the lap samples (or lap plus teleport, depending on the session), subtract `0.7 * Fneu`, add each trial's mean neuropil back so the ratio is a true dF/F, take a maximin baseline (Gaussian smoothing with sigma 15 samples, then a 300-sample running minimum followed by a 300-sample running maximum, the Methods' 20 s window), form `(F - baseline)/|baseline|`, smooth with a 2-sample Gaussian, and deconvolve with OASIS at `tau = 0.7` and `frame_rate/n_planes`. Cells from multiple planes are pooled afterwards.\n   120→\n   121→Whether the baseline window may span the teleport is a per-mouse, per-day fact, copied from `teleport_metadata.py`: the laser was blanked between trials except on the days listed there, where imaging continued. It matters -- on m15 day 5, spanning the teleport widens the baseline window by 35% and moves the emitted events by ~9% (median per-cell r = 0.986).\n   122→\n   123→ii.\n   124→```python\n   125→dff_curr, events_curr = dff(fluorescence_curr.T.astype(np.float64),\n   126→                            pd.Series(trial_start_idx + 1), pd.Series(trial_end_idx + 1),\n   127→                            f_neu=neuropil_curr.T.astype(np.float64),\n   128→                            neuropil_method='subtract', baseline_method='maximin',\n   129→                            subtract_baseline=True, neu_coef=0.7, tau=0.7,\n   130→                            frame_rate=frame_rate, n_planes=nplanes, deconvolve=True,\n   131→                            keep_teleports=keep_teleports)\n   132→```\n   133→\n   134→iii. This is the processing the Methods describe: \"baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window ... dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p.\" The parameters are theirs: `neu_coef = 0.7` and `baseline_method = 'maximin'` from `notebooks/make_multi_anim_sess.ipynb`, `tau = 0.7` from the suite2p ops in `notebooks/suite2p_notebooks/example_m12.ipynb`.\n   135→\n   136→The neural sampling rate is the same for all recordings once the multi-plane sessions are accounted for. An assertion checks that the alignment error is within half a time bin.\n   279→\n   280→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Previous trial outcome*?\n   281→\n   282→i. For each trial, check whether any reward event occurred in the *previous* trial's time range. For the first trial, set to 0. The value is constant across all timepoints within a trial.\n   283→\n   284→ii.\n   285→```python\n   286→if trial == 0:\n   287→    input_curr[3,:] = 0\n   288→else:\n   289→    prev_reward_idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial-1], trial_end_idx[trial-1], dtype=int)\n   290→    isprevreward = np.any(isreward[prev_reward_idx])\n   291→    input_curr[3,:] = int(isprevreward)\n   292→```\n   293→\n   294→iii. The instructions specify previous trial outcome as binary (omitted=0, rewarded=1). The code checks if any reward event fell within the previous trial's time boundaries.\n   295→\n   296→## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Distance to reward zone* derived from?\n   297→\n   298→i. Derived from the `position` behavior time series and the reward zone location for the current trial. The reward zone boundaries come from the `reward_zone` behavior time series. This variable is non-zero when the mouse is in the reward zone. I combine this with the position of the mouse when this is non-zero to figure out the closest reward zone. To allow for some noise (of unknown source) and unrewarded tasks, I use the Viterbi algorithm to assign zones so that the rewarded positions match the known reward zones and it is uncommon to switch reward zone. \n   299→\n   300→ii. Assigning reward zone location for each trial: \n   301→- Before converting, the `survey()` function was used to try to understand all the data values, including whatever is going on with the `reward_zone` variable. It first computes the position of the min and max position of the mouse when `reward_zone` is non-zero:\n   302→```python\n   303→behavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \n   304→info['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\n   305→position = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\n   306→for trial in range(ntrials):\n   307→    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n   308→    pos_trial = position[idx]\n   309→    reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n   310→    if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n   311→        continue\n   312→    info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n   313→```\n   314→The function `segment_reward_positions()` uses the Viterbi algorithm to assign reward zone labels so that the initial rewarded positions are close to the corresponding rewarded zone:\n   315→```python\n   316→reward_zone_dict = {'A': [80, 130],\n   317→                    'B': [200, 250],\n   318→                    'C': [320, 370]}\n   319→...\n   320→sigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\n   321→zone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\n   322→dist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n   323→                    startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\n   324→dist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\n   325→log_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n   326→```\n   327→It also encourages the reward zone label to not change often:\n   328→```python\n   329→pstay = .9\n   330→log_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\n   331→np.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n   332→```\n   333→\n   334→iii. The paper defines reward zones A, B, C at specific position ranges. These same ranges could also be found, with some translation, in the code (required mapping X -> A, etc.). I plotted the position of the mouse when `reward_zone` was positive (note that the value was between 0 and 6 I think) to determine that the Viterbi approach would likely work, and that the start of the rewarded position was the least noisy variable to use. The class fractions were approximately 1/3 after this, so it seemed correct. \n   335→\n   336→## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n   337→\n   338→i. For each timepoint, compute the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone. Distance is 0 when inside the zone, negative when before the zone, and positive when past it.  The continuous distance is then discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n   339→\n   340→ii.\n   341→```python\n   342→def compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n   343→    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n   344→    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n   345→    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n   346→    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n   347→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0]\n   348→    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n   349→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1]\n   350→    return distance_to_reward_zone\n   351→\n   352→```\n   353→```python\n   354→distance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n   355→...\n   356→output_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n   357→```\n   358→\n   359→\n   360→iii. This matches the paper's concept of distance relative to the reward zone. The reward zone boundaries are from `reward_zone_dict`, inspired by the reference code `Sosa_et_al_2024/src/reward_relative/behavior.py`. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n   361→\n   362→## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n   363→\n   364→i. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n   365→\n   366→ii.\n   367→```python\n   368→distance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n   369→...\n   370→output_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n   371→```\n   372→\n   373→iii. The bin edges match the instructions. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n   374→\n   375→## 7-d. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n   376→\n   377→i. The position data and neural data share the same time indices within each trial, so no additional alignment is needed.\n   378→\n   379→ii.\n   380→```python\n   381→idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n   382→spike_data_curr = spike_data[idx]\n   383→position_curr = position[idx]\n   384→```\n   385→\n   386→iii. Both neural and behavioral data use the same time indexing (verified by timestamp assertions earlier in the code), so indexing by the same `idx` array ensures alignment.\n   387→\n   388→## 8-a. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n   525→\n   526→i. Several cases are handled:\n   527→- **Neural/behavior length mismatch**: If the number of neural time bins differs from behavior timestamps, data is cropped to the minimum of the two with a warning printed.\n   528→- **Short trials**: Trials with fewer than 50 timepoints (`min_ntimepoints`) are skipped with a warning.\n   529→- **Missing reward zone data**: Trials where `reward_zone` is never active (all zeros) get NaN positions, and the Viterbi algorithm handles these via uniform emission (assignment driven by neighboring trials' transitions).\n   530→- **Reward timestamp alignment**: An assertion verifies reward times align within half a time bin of behavior timestamps.\n   531→\n   532→ii.\n   533→```python\n   534→if T != len(timestamps):\n   535→    print(f\"Warning: number of neural time bins ({T}) does not match number of behavior timestamps ({len(timestamps)}), cropping to the minimum of the two\")\n   536→    T = min(T, len(timestamps))\n   537→    spike_data = spike_data[:T]\n   538→    timestamps = timestamps[:T]\n   539→...\n   540→if idx.sum() < min_ntimepoints:\n   541→    print(f\"Skipping trial {trial}...\")\n   542→    istrial[trial] = False\n   543→    continue\n   544→```\n   545→\n   546→iii. These are defensive checks found during data exploration. The neural/behavior mismatch occurs in a small number of sessions.\n   547→\n   548→## 13-a. Looking at trial lengths, there were a couple short trials that this removed, at some version of the code. \n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from? \n\ni. Neural data is derived from the raw suite2p traces, `Fluorescence` (F) and `Neuropil` (Fneu). It is **not** the NWB's `Deconvolved` field, which is a different signal that the paper never analyzes.\n\nii. \n```python\nfluorescence_data = nwbfile.processing[\"ophys\"][\"Fluorescence\"]\nneuropil_data = nwbfile.processing[\"ophys\"][\"Neuropil\"]\nfor plane_name, plane_data in fluorescence_data.roi_response_series.items():\n    fluorescence_curr = plane_data.data[:]\n    neuropil_curr = neuropil_data.roi_response_series[plane_name].data[:]\n```\n\niii. The paper computes its own neural signal rather than using a stored one in `preprocessing.dff(F, Fneu, ..., deconvolve=True)` and stores the result as `sess.timeseries['events']`. The NWB's `Deconvolved` array is instead suite2p's own deconvolution of raw fluorescence.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed? \n\ni. dF/F is computed from F and Fneu and then deconvolved into \"events\", by a copy of the paper's own `preprocessing.dff`. Per plane: restrict to the lap samples (or lap plus teleport, depending on the session), subtract `0.7 * Fneu`, add each trial's mean neuropil back so the ratio is a true dF/F, take a maximin baseline (Gaussian smoothing with sigma 15 samples, then a 300-sample running minimum followed by a 300-sample running maximum, the Methods' 20 s window), form `(F - baseline)/|baseline|`, smooth with a 2-sample Gaussian, and deconvolve with OASIS at `tau = 0.7` and `frame_rate/n_planes`. Cells from multiple planes are pooled afterwards.\n\nWhether the baseline window may span the teleport is a per-mouse, per-day fact, copied from `teleport_metadata.py`: the laser was blanked between trials except on the days listed there, where imaging continued. It matters -- on m15 day 5, spanning the teleport widens the baseline window by 35% and moves the emitted events by ~9% (median per-cell r = 0.986).\n\nii.\n```python\ndff_curr, events_curr = dff(fluorescence_curr.T.astype(np.float64),\n                            pd.Series(trial_start_idx + 1), pd.Series(trial_end_idx + 1),\n                            f_neu=neuropil_curr.T.astype(np.float64),\n                            neuropil_method='subtract', baseline_method='maximin',\n                            subtract_baseline=True, neu_coef=0.7, tau=0.7,\n                            frame_rate=frame_rate, n_planes=nplanes, deconvolve=True,\n                            keep_teleports=keep_teleports)\n```\n\niii. This is the processing the Methods describe: \"baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window ... dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p.\" The parameters are theirs: `neu_coef = 0.7` and `baseline_method = 'maximin'` from `notebooks/make_multi_anim_sess.ipynb`, `tau = 0.7` from the suite2p ops in `notebooks/suite2p_notebooks/example_m12.ipynb`.\n\nThe neural sampling rate is the same for all recordings once the multi-plane sessions are accounted for. Note that the `rate` stored on the NWB series is the scanner rate, so on the two-plane sessions it is 31 Hz and the per-plane rate -- the one that sets both the time bin and the deconvolution kernel -- is `rate/nplanes`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two filters, both the paper's. Cells are first restricted to the `iscell` ROIs, which carry suite2p's manual curation. Putative interneurons are then dropped: any cell whose dF/F correlates with the animal's running speed at r > 0.5.\n\nii. \n```python\niscell = np.array(roi_data['iscell'].to_list())[:,0] == 1\nfluorescence.append(fluorescence_curr[:,iscell])\nneuropil.append(neuropil_curr[:,iscell])\n...\nisint = is_putative_interneuron(dff_curr, speed)\nspike_data.append(events_curr[~isint].T)\n```\n\niii. `iscell` is the manual curation the Methods describe. The per-session counts approximately matches ranges in the paper. The second filter is the Methods' additional step of filtering putative interneurons. \n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`? \n\ni. The data are the be aligned with the trial start, which doesn't require any additional processing beyond splitting into trials. \n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 2-e. Timestamps are not available for the neural data, just a stored `rate`. This rate is the same for all recordings, once one accounts for the multi-plane recordings\n\n## 3-a. Behavior timestamps were consistent with the constant neural data rate, many choices would have resulted in the same result. \n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds*?\n\ni. Common sense\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* aligned with the neural data? \n\ni. The trial number is simply the sequential index of the trial within the session.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Previous trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `Reward` behavior time series. Reward event timestamps are matched to behavior timestamps, and a binary `isreward` array marks timepoints where rewards occurred.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_amounts = behavior_data['Reward'].data[:][:T]\nreward_times = behavior_data['Reward'].timestamps[:]\nreward_time_indices = np.searchsorted(timestamps, reward_times)\nisreward = np.zeros(T)\nisreward[reward_time_indices] = 1\n```\n\niii. The `Reward` time series has its own timestamps (not aligned to the behavior sampling rate), so `searchsorted` is used to find the nearest timepoint. An assertion checks that the alignment error is within half a time bin.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Previous trial outcome*?\n\ni. For each trial, check whether any reward event occurred in the *previous* trial's time range. For the first trial, set to 0. The value is constant across all timepoints within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nif trial == 0:\n    input_curr[3,:] = 0\nelse:\n    prev_reward_idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial-1], trial_end_idx[trial-1], dtype=int)\n    isprevreward = np.any(isreward[prev_reward_idx])\n    input_curr[3,:] = int(isprevreward)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify previous trial outcome as binary (omitted=0, rewarded=1). The code checks if any reward event fell within the previous trial's time boundaries.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Distance to reward zone* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavior time series and the reward zone location for the current trial. The reward zone boundaries come from the `reward_zone` behavior time series. This variable is non-zero when the mouse is in the reward zone. I combine this with the position of the mouse when this is non-zero to figure out the closest reward zone. To allow for some noise (of unknown source) and unrewarded tasks, I use the Viterbi algorithm to assign zones so that the rewarded positions match the known reward zones and it is uncommon to switch reward zone. \n\nii. Assigning reward zone location for each trial: \n- Before converting, the `survey()` function was used to try to understand all the data values, including whatever is going on with the `reward_zone` variable. It first computes the position of the min and max position of the mouse when `reward_zone` is non-zero:\n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \ninfo['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\nposition = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    pos_trial = position[idx]\n    reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n    if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n        continue\n    info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n```\nThe function `segment_reward_positions()` uses the Viterbi algorithm to assign reward zone labels so that the initial rewarded positions are close to the corresponding rewarded zone:\n```python\nreward_zone_dict = {'A': [80, 130],\n                    'B': [200, 250],\n                    'C': [320, 370]}\n...\nsigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\nzone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\ndist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n                    startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\ndist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\nlog_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n```\nIt also encourages the reward zone label to not change often:\n```python\npstay = .9\nlog_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\nnp.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n```\n\niii. The paper defines reward zones A, B, C at specific position ranges. These same ranges could also be found, with some translation, in the code (required mapping X -> A, etc.). I plotted the position of the mouse when `reward_zone` was positive (note that the value was between 0 and 6 I think) to determine that the Viterbi approach would likely work, and that the start of the rewarded position was the least noisy variable to use. The class fractions were approximately 1/3 after this, so it seemed correct. \n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n\ni. For each timepoint, compute the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone. Distance is 0 when inside the zone, negative when before the zone, and positive when past it.  The continuous distance is then discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0]\n    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1]\n    return distance_to_reward_zone\n\n```\n```python\ndistance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n```\n\n\niii. This matches the paper's concept of distance relative to the reward zone. The reward zone boundaries are from `reward_zone_dict`, inspired by the reference code `Sosa_et_al_2024/src/reward_relative/behavior.py`. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndistance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n```\n\niii. The bin edges match the instructions. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The position data and neural data share the same time indices within each trial, so no additional alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\nidx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\nspike_data_curr = spike_data[idx]\nposition_curr = position[idx]\n```\n\niii. No processing beyond extracting the per-trial slice and discretizing. Discretized into 5 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, 90, 180, 270, 360, inf]`, corresponding to 90 cm wide bins spanning the 450 cm track. Within the converted trials the position ranges from -0.8 to 451.8 cm, so the open first and last bins absorb the few samples that fall just outside the track (185 of 1,307,665). \n\nii.\n```python\nposition_curr = position[idx]\noutput_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n```\n\n```python\nposition_bins = [-np.inf,90,180,270,360,np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n```\n\n\niii. The raw position values are used directly. Each lap starts at 0 cm and the track is 450 cm long (Methods), so five 90 cm bins over 0 to 450 are equal-sized by construction. The survey's wider -50 to 450 range comes from including the inter-trial teleport period, which is not part of any converted trial.\n\n## 8-c. Discretized into 5 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, 90, 180, 270, 360, inf]`, corresponding to 90 cm wide bins spanning the 450 cm track. \n\nii.\n```python\nposition_bins = [-np.inf,90,180,270,360,np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n```\n\niii. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several cases are handled:\n- **Neural/behavior length mismatch**: If the number of neural time bins differs from behavior timestamps, data is cropped to the minimum of the two with a warning printed.\n- **Short trials**: Trials with fewer than 50 timepoints (`min_ntimepoints`) are skipped with a warning.\n- **Missing reward zone data**: Trials where `reward_zone` is never active (all zeros) get NaN positions, and the Viterbi algorithm handles these via uniform emission (assignment driven by neighboring trials' transitions).\n- **Reward timestamp alignment**: An assertion verifies reward times align within half a time bin of behavior timestamps.\n\nii.\n```python\nif T != len(timestamps):\n    print(f\"Warning: number of neural time bins ({T}) does not match number of behavior timestamps ({len(timestamps)}), cropping to the minimum of the two\")\n    T = min(T, len(timestamps))\n    spike_data = spike_data[:T]\n    timestamps = timestamps[:T]\n...\nif idx.sum() < min_ntimepoints:\n    print(f\"Skipping trial {trial}...\")\n    istrial[trial] = False\n    continue\n```\n\niii. These are defensive checks found during data exploration. The neural/behavior mismatch occurs in a small number of sessions.\n\n## 13-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming steps are:\n1. **Loading NWB files** (`pynwb.NWBHDF5IO` and reading large arrays) — I/O bound\n2. **The survey step** — loads every NWB file to gather statistics and reward zone positions\n3. **Full conversion** — loads every NWB file again and extracts all trial data\n4. **Saving the .pkl file**\n\nii. The per-trial loop in `convert_session_data` (lines 267-332) iterates over each trial sequentially. Some operations like `np.digitize` and `compute_distance_to_reward_zone` could theoretically be applied to the full session arrays before splitting into trials, but the variable trial lengths make this awkward. The Viterbi inner loop (lines 503-507) iterates over states, which could be vectorized with matrix operations.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The per-trial loop is the natural structure given variable-length trials. Vectorizing would require padding or masking.\n\n## 13-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. The survey step and the conversion step both load every NWB file and read the same data arrays (neural, behavior). The survey reads neural data to count neurons and check rates, then the conversion reads it again. The `reward_zone_positions` are computed in the survey and then used in the conversion, but other data (position, speed, lick, etc.) is read twice. \n\nii. N/A\n\niii. I don't think these could be run at the same time, as it would require too much RAM. The Viterbi output created during surveying is required to process the data. It was convenient to run the survey before deciding how to handle the conversion of the data. \n\n## 13-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. neural signal processing, COPIED from the paper's code repo\n    61→#\n    62→# The NWB \"Deconvolved\" array is NOT the signal the paper analyses. It is suite2p's own \n    63→# deconvolution of raw fluorescence, and not used in the paper analyses. \n    64→# =================================================================================================\n    65→\n    66→# Experiment days on which the laser was NOT blanked between trials, so the dF/F baseline window is\n    67→# allowed to span the teleport. Animals missing from this table (m3, m4 and m7) were\n    68→# blanked on every day, so their baselines are always restricted to the lap.\n    69→# ** copied from src/reward_relative/teleport_metadata.py::teleport_sessions **\n    70→\n    71→# Dictionary of animals and experiment days for which the teleport periods were imaged\n    72→# (as opposed to blanking the laser)\n    73→\n    74→teleport_sessions = {\n    75→    'GCAMP10': {'exp_days': [1, 7, 8, 14, 15]},\n    76→    'GCAMP11': {'exp_days': [1, 7, 8, 14, 15]},\n    77→    'GCAMP12': {'exp_days': [1, 7, 8, 14, 15]},\n    78→    'GCAMP13': {'exp_days': [1, 7, 8, 14, 15]},\n    79→    'GCAMP14': {'exp_days': [1, 7, 8, 14, 15]},\n    80→    'GCAMP15': {'exp_days': [1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12, 14, 15, 16, 17]},\n    81→    'GCAMP17': {'exp_days': [1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12, 14, 15, 16, 17]},\n    82→    'GCAMP18': {'exp_days': [1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12, 14, 15, 16, 17]},\n    83→    'GCAMP19': {'exp_days': [1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12, 14, 15, 16, 17]},\n    84→}\n    85→\n    86→\n    87→def nansmooth(a, sig):\n    88→    \"\"\"\n    89→    apply Gaussian smoothing to matrix A containing nans with kernel sig\n    90→    without propagating nans\n    91→    :param a:\n    92→    :param sig:\n    93→    :return:\n    94→\n    95→    ** copied from TwoPUtils/utilities.py::nansmooth **\n    96→    \"\"\"\n    97→\n    98→    # find nans\n    99→    nan_inds = np.isnan(a)\n   100→    a_nanless = np.copy(a)\n   101→    # make nans 0\n   102→    a_nanless[nan_inds] = 0\n   103→\n   104→    # inversely weight nanned indices\n   105→    one = np.ones(a.shape)\n   106→    one[nan_inds] = .001\n   107→    a_nanless = ndimage.gaussian_filter(a_nanless, sig) # was filters.gaussian_filter\n   108→    one = ndimage.gaussian_filter(one, sig) # was filters.gaussian_filter\n   109→    return a_nanless / one\n   110→\n   111→\n   112→def nansmooth_1d(a, sig, axis=-1, return_nans=False, **kwargs):\n   113→    \"\"\"\n   114→    apply Gaussian smoothing to matrix A containing nans with kernel sig\n   115→    without propagating nans\n   116→    :param a:\n   117→    :param sig:\n   118→    :return:\n   119→\n   120→    ** copied from src/reward_relative/utilities.py::nansmooth **\n   121→    (renamed: the TwoPUtils N-D version above already occupies the name `nansmooth`)\n   122→    \"\"\"\n   123→\n   124→    # find nans\n   125→    nan_inds = np.isnan(a)\n   126→    a_nanless = np.copy(a)\n   127→    # make nans 0\n   128→    a_nanless[nan_inds] = 0\n   129→\n   130→    # inversely weight nanned indices\n   131→    one = np.ones(a.shape)\n   132→    one[nan_inds] = 0.001\n   133→    a_nanless = ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(a_nanless, sig, axis=axis, **kwargs) # was filters.gaussian_filter\n   134→    one = ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(one, sig, axis=axis, **kwargs) # was filters.gaussian_filter\n   135→\n   136→    out = a_nanless / one\n   137→    if return_nans:\n   138→        out[nan_inds] = np.nan\n   139→\n   140→    return out\n   141→\n   142→def dff(f,\n   143→        trial_starts,\n   144→        teleports,\n   145→        f_neu=None,\n   146→        neuropil_method=None,\n   147→        baseline_method='maximin',\n   148→        subtract_baseline=True,\n   149→        neu_coef=0.7,\n   150→        tau=0.7,\n   151→        frame_rate=15,\n   152→        n_planes=1,\n   153→        deconvolve=False,\n   154→        keep_teleports=False):\n   155→    \"\"\"\n   156→    Calculate dFF for 1 channel\n   157→\n   158→    :param f: ROI fluorescence timeseries\n   159→    :param trial_starts:\n   160→    :param teleports:\n   161→    :param f_neu: neuropil fluorescence timeseries\n   162→    :param baseline_method:\n   163→    :param subtract_baseline: True gives ∆F/F, False just normalizes F to the basline (divides only)\n   164→    :param neu_coef:\n   165→    :param deconvolve: whether to deconvolve the dff trace into \"events\" (psuedo spikes)\n   166→    :return: dff and spks if deconvolve is True, otherwise just dff\n   167→\n   168→    `trial_starts` and `teleports` must be pandas Series: the keep_teleports branch calls\n   169→    `.to_list()` on `teleports`, which a numpy array does not have. That is their code, unchanged.\n   170→\n   171→    ** copied from src/reward_relative/preprocessing.py::dff **\n   172→    Keeps only the branches this dataset can reach (single-channel)\n   173→    Dropped: bleedthrough_ts, neu_bleedthrough_ts, regress_ts, scrub_ts, neuropil_method='regress',\n   174→    baseline_method='maxsmooth', subtract_baseline=False. \n   175→    \"\"\"\n   176→\n   177→    print('baseline method =', baseline_method)\n   178→\n   179→    # Check frame rate if deconvolving\n   180→    if deconvolve:\n   181→        if (frame_rate/n_planes) > 16:\n   182→            warnings.warn(\n   183→                f\"Using frame rate per plane: {(frame_rate/n_planes)} -- is this correct?\")\n   184→\n   185→    f_ = np.zeros(f.shape) * np.nan\n   186→\n   187→    if f_neu is not None:\n   188→        f_neu_ = np.zeros(f_neu.shape) * np.nan\n   189→\n   190→    if keep_teleports:\n   191→        # if keeping the teleports, keep all the fluorescence from the start of imaging\n   192→        # EXCEPT the teleport sample itself, as this position values might be interpolated between\n   193→        # the end of the track and -50 (where the teleport jitter starts)\n   194→        print(\"keeping teleports\")\n   195→        start_inds = np.append(\n   196→            trial_starts[0],\n   197→            np.array(teleports[:-1] + 2)\n   198→        ).tolist()\n   199→        # ^ add 2 so with start-1 we will start 1 sample after the teleport\n   200→        stop_inds = teleports.to_list()\n   201→        # we will subtract 1 off this index below\n   202→    else:\n   203→        # if not keeping the teleports, keep only the fluorescence on each trial\n   204→        start_inds = trial_starts.tolist()\n   205→        stop_inds = teleports.tolist()\n   206→\n   207→    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(start_inds, stop_inds)):\n   208→        f_[:, start - 1:stop - 1] = f[:, start - 1:stop - 1]\n   209→\n   210→        if f_neu is not None:\n   211→            f_neu_[:, start - 1:stop - 1] = f_neu[:, start - 1:stop - 1]\n   212→\n   213→    nanmask = ~np.isnan(f_[0, :])\n   214→\n   215→    # once bleedthrough is subtracted, do neuropil correction\n   216→\n   217→    if neuropil_method == 'subtract':\n   218→        f_ -= neu_coef * f_neu_\n   219→\n   220→    print('No minimum subtraction')\n   221→\n   222→    # Caluclate baseline for chan 1\n   223→    flow = np.zeros(f_.shape) * np.nan\n   224→    spks = np.zeros(f_.shape) * np.nan\n   225→\n   226→    # get baseline within each trial\n   227→    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(start_inds, stop_inds)):\n   228→\n   229→        # if we subtracted neuropil, add back in the neuropil mean in each trial\n   230→        # such that dff values will be close to true ∆F/F, and we don't divide by small numbers\n   231→        if neuropil_method == 'subtract':\n   232→            f_[:, start-1:stop-1] = f_[:, start-1:stop-1] + neu_coef * np.nanmean(\n   233→                f_neu_[:, start-1:stop-1], axis=1, keepdims=True)\n   234→\n   235→        if baseline_method == 'maximin':\n   236→            # cut out ITIs and smooth signal\n   237→            flow[:, start - 1:stop -\n   238→                 1] = nansmooth(f_[:, start-1:stop-1], [0, 15])\n   239→\n   240→            # minimum filter, taking min val over 20 sec\n   241→            flow[:, start-1:stop-1] = ndimage.minimum_filter1d(\n   242→                flow[:, start-1:stop-1], int(300), axis=-1) # was sp.ndimage.filters.minimum_filter1d\n   243→            flow[:, start-1:stop-1] = ndimage.maximum_filter1d(\n   244→                flow[:, start-1:stop-1], int(300), axis=-1)  # max filter with same window (dilation), was sp.ndimage.filters.maximum_filter1d\n   245→\n   246→    # to get deltaF/F: subtract baseline from initial signal, divide by abs(baseline)\n   247→    # baseline can some times end up as negative due to regression\n   248→    dff = np.zeros(f_.shape) * np.nan\n   249→    if subtract_baseline:\n   250→        dff[:, nanmask] = (f_[:, nanmask] - flow[:, nanmask]\n   251→                           ) / np.abs(flow[:, nanmask])\n   252→\n   253→    print('dff:', dff.shape, 'min ', np.nanmin(dff), 'max ', np.nanmax(dff))\n   254→\n   255→    # Smooth the deltaF/F transients by 2 time bins\n   256→    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(start_inds, stop_inds)):\n   257→        dff[:, start - 1:stop -\n   258→            1] = nansmooth_1d(dff[:, start - 1:stop - 1], 2, axis=1)\n   259→        # After smoothing: Get \"spikes\" with calcium kernel deconvolution from suite2p\n   260→        if deconvolve:\n   261→            spks[:, start-1:stop-1] = dcnv.oasis(\n   262→                np.ascontiguousarray(dff[:, start-1:stop-1], dtype=np.float32),\n   263→                2000, tau, (frame_rate/n_planes)) # this is float64 in the original code\n   264→\n   265→    if deconvolve:\n   266→        return dff, spks\n   267→    else:\n   268→        return dff\n   269→\n   270→\n   271→def is_putative_interneuron(dff, speed, r_thresh=0.5):\n   272→    \"\"\"\n   273→    Find putative interneurons based on correlation of dF/F with speed\n   274→    (under the hypothesis that fast-spiking interneurons in the pyramidal\n   275→    layer are often highly correlated with running speed).\n   276→\n   277→    :param dff: (ncells, T) dF/F timeseries, NaN outside the analysed windows\n   278→    :param speed: (T,) running speed in cm/s\n   279→    :param r_thresh: threshold for speed correlation r; dayData sets int_thresh = 0.5, which is\n   280→        the value the Methods quote (\"a Pearson correlation of >0.5\"). Note that the threshold\n   281→        defaulted to 0.3 in the source function; the analyses always pass 0.5.\n   282→    :return: is_int (array) with a Boolean for each ROI, where putative interneuron is True.\n   283→\n   284→    ** copied from src/reward_relative/spatial.py::is_putative_interneuron **\n   285→    Change: takes the dF/F and speed arrays as arguments instead of reading them off a TwoPUtils \n   286→    `sess` object, which this conversion does not build.\n   287→    \"\"\"\n   288→    speed_corr = np.zeros((dff.shape[0],))\n   289→    nanmask = ~np.isnan(dff[0, :])\n   290→\n   291→    for c in range(dff.shape[0]):\n   292→        speed_corr[c] = np.corrcoef(\n   293→            dff[c, nanmask], speed[nanmask])[0, 1]\n   294→\n   295→    is_int = speed_corr > r_thresh\n   296→\n   297→    return is_int\n   298→\n   299→# end copy-paste\n   300→# =================================================================================================\n   301→\n   302→\n   303→def get_subjects(datadir=DATADIR):\n   304→    # find all directories in datadir that start with 'sub-'\n   305→    subjects = [d.split('-')[1] for d in os.listdir(datadir) if d.startswith('sub-')]\n   306→    return subjects\n   307→\n   308→def get_sessions(subject, datadir=DATADIR):\n   309→    subject_dir = os.path.join(datadir, f\"sub-{subject}\")\n   310→    # parse sessions from files named sub-<subject>-<session>_behavior+ophys.nwb\n   311→    nwb_files = [f for f in os.listdir(subject_dir) if f.endswith('.nwb')]\n   312→    sessions = [int(f.split('_')[1].split('-')[1]) for f in nwb_files]\n   313→    return sessions\n   314→\n   315→def get_nwb_file(subject, session, datadir=DATADIR):\n   316→    nwb_file_path = os.path.join(datadir, f\"sub-{subject}\", f\"sub-{subject}_ses-{session:02d}_behavior+ophys.nwb\")\n   317→    assert os.path.exists(nwb_file_path), f\"File {nwb_file_path} does not exist\"\n   318→    return nwb_file_path\n   319→\n   320→def interp(new_idx,old_idx,old_data,**kwargs):\n   321→    f = interp1d(old_idx, old_data, axis=0, **kwargs)\n   322→    return f(new_idx)\n   323→\n   324→\n   325→def get_trial_starts_ends(trial_start,teleport):\n   326→    trial_start_idx = np.nonzero(trial_start)[0]\n   327→    is_teleport_start = (teleport[1:] > 0) & (teleport[:-1] <= 0)\n   328→    trial_end_idx = np.nonzero(is_teleport_start)[0] + 1 \n   329→    assert len(trial_start_idx) == len(trial_end_idx), f\"Number of trial starts ({len(trial_start_idx)}) does not match number of trial ends ({len(trial_end_idx)})\"\n   330→    return trial_start_idx, trial_end_idx\n   331→        \n   332→\n   333→def survey_session_data(subject, session, datadir=DATADIR):\n   334→    nwb_file_path = get_nwb_file(subject, session, datadir)\n   335→    info = {}\n   336→    info['reward_zone_positions'] = []\n   337→    with pynwb.NWBHDF5IO(nwb_file_path, 'r') as io:\n   338→        nwbfile = io.read()\n   339→        # parse neural activity data\n   340→        deconvolved_data = nwbfile.processing[\"ophys\"][\"Deconvolved\"]\n   341→        spike_data = []\n   342→        nplanes = len(deconvolved_data.roi_response_series)\n   343→        for plane_data in deconvolved_data.roi_response_series.values():\n   344→            roi_data = plane_data.rois[:]\n   345→            iscell = np.array(roi_data['iscell'].to_list())[:,0] == 1\n   346→            spike_data_curr = plane_data.data[:]\n   347→            assert spike_data_curr.shape[1] == iscell.shape[0], f\"Number of neurons in data ({spike_data_curr.shape[1]}) does not match number of iscell entries ({iscell.shape[0]})\"\n   348→            spike_data.append(spike_data_curr[:,iscell])\n   349→        spike_data = np.concatenate(spike_data, axis=1)\n   350→        info['T'], info['nneurons'] = spike_data.shape\n   351→        max_spike_rate = np.max(spike_data)\n   352→        info['max_spike_rate_range'] = (max_spike_rate.min(), max_spike_rate.max())\n   353→        info['neural_time_bin_size'] = nplanes/plane_data.rate*1000 # ms\n   354→\n   355→        behavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \n   356→        istrial = behavior_data['trial number'].data[:] >= 0\n   357→        # reward_amounts are all the same\n   358→        reward_amounts = behavior_data['Reward'].data[:]\n   359→        info['unique_reward_amounts'] = np.unique(reward_amounts)\n   360→        info['nrewards'] = len(reward_amounts)\n   361→        # autoreward is all 0s, so not useful for modeling, but check that it is all zeros\n   362→        autoreward = behavior_data['autoreward'].data[:]\n   363→        info['unique_autoreward'] = np.unique(autoreward[istrial])\n   364→        # vr environment\n   365→        vr_environment = behavior_data['environment'].data[:] # one value per time point, does this change by session?\n   366→        info['unique_vr_environment'] = np.unique(vr_environment[istrial])\n   367→        licks = behavior_data['lick'].data[:]\n   368→        info['unique_licks'] = np.unique(licks[istrial])\n   369→        position = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\n   370→        info['position_range'] = (position[istrial].min(), position[istrial].max())\n   371→        reward_zone = behavior_data['reward_zone'].data[:]\n   372→        info['unique_reward_zone'] = np.unique(reward_zone[istrial])\n   373→        scanning = behavior_data['scanning'].data[:]\n   374→        info['unique_scanning'] = np.unique(scanning[istrial])\n   375→        speed = behavior_data['speed'].data[:]\n   376→        info['speed_range'] = (speed[istrial].min(), speed[istrial].max())\n   377→        teleport = behavior_data['teleport'].data[:]\n   378→        info['unique_teleport'] = np.unique(teleport[istrial])\n   379→        \n   380→        # trial number and trial start do not agree with each other\n   381→        # use trial_start and teleport to find starts and ends of trials\n   382→        trial_start_idx, trial_end_idx = get_trial_starts_ends(behavior_data['trial_start'].data[:], teleport)\n   383→        trial_length = trial_end_idx - trial_start_idx\n   384→        info['trial_length_range'] = (trial_length.min(), trial_length.max())\n   385→\n   386→        ntrials = len(trial_start_idx)\n   387→        info['ntrials'] = ntrials\n   388→        info['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\n   389→        for trial in range(ntrials):\n   390→            idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n   391→            pos_trial = position[idx]\n   392→            reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n   393→            if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n   394→                continue\n   395→            info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n   396→                    \n   397→    return info\n   398→    \n   399→def get_bin_centers(bin_edges,data):\n   400→    # replace infinity with the max/min of the data for plotting purposes\n   401→    bin_edges_plot = np.array(bin_edges.copy())\n   402→    if np.isinf(bin_edges_plot[0]):\n   403→        bin_edges_plot[0] = data.min()\n   404→        if bin_edges_plot[0] > bin_edges_plot[1]:\n   405→            bin_edges_plot[0] = 2*bin_edges_plot[1] - bin_edges_plot[2] # extrapolate to get a lower value for the first bin edge\n   406→    if np.isinf(bin_edges_plot[-1]):\n   407→        bin_edges_plot[-1] = data.max()\n   408→        if bin_edges_plot[-1] < bin_edges_plot[-2]:\n   409→            bin_edges_plot[-1] = 2*bin_edges_plot[-2] - bin_edges_plot[-3] # extrapolate to get a higher value for the last bin edge\n   410→    bin_centers = (bin_edges_plot[:-1] + bin_edges_plot[1:]) / 2\n   411→    return bin_centers\n   412→    \n   413→def compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n   414→    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n   415→    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n   416→    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n   417→    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n   418→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0] # negative if before the zone\n   419→    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n   420→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1] # positive if after the zone\n   421→    return distance_to_reward_zone\n   422→    \n   423→def convert_session_data(subject, session, info, trial_offset, time_bin_size=None, datadir=DATADIR, \n   424→                         show_processing=False, max_neurons_plot=30, min_ntimepoints=50,\n   425→                         figfile=None):\n   426→\n   427→    nwb_file_path = get_nwb_file(subject, session, datadir)\n   428→    io = pynwb.NWBHDF5IO(nwb_file_path, 'r')\n   429→    nwbfile = io.read()\n   430→\n   431→    # parse neural activity data: the raw suite2p traces the paper computes its dF/F and\n   432→    # deconvolved events from. The deconvolution needs the trial boundaries, so it runs further\n   433→    # below, once those have been parsed out of the behavior data.\n   434→    fluorescence_data = nwbfile.processing[\"ophys\"][\"Fluorescence\"]\n   435→    neuropil_data = nwbfile.processing[\"ophys\"][\"Neuropil\"]\n   436→    fluorescence = []\n   437→    neuropil = []\n   438→    nplanes = len(fluorescence_data.roi_response_series)\n   439→    for plane_name, plane_data in fluorescence_data.roi_response_series.items():\n   440→        roi_data = plane_data.rois[:]\n   441→        iscell = np.array(roi_data['iscell'].to_list())[:,0] == 1\n   442→        fluorescence_curr = plane_data.data[:]\n   443→        neuropil_curr = neuropil_data.roi_response_series[plane_name].data[:]\n   444→        assert fluorescence_curr.shape[1] == iscell.shape[0], f\"Number of neurons in data ({fluorescence_curr.shape[1]}) does not match number of iscell entries ({iscell.shape[0]})\"\n   445→        assert neuropil_curr.shape == fluorescence_curr.shape, f\"Neuropil shape ({neuropil_curr.shape}) does not match fluorescence shape ({fluorescence_curr.shape})\"\n   446→        # Subset to the curated cells now rather than after dff. Every step of dff is per-cell --\n   447→        # its only cross-cell operation is a Gaussian with sigma 0 on the cell axis -- so this is\n   448→        # numerically identical to subsetting afterwards, on a third of the memory.\n   449→        fluorescence.append(fluorescence_curr[:,iscell])\n   450→        neuropil.append(neuropil_curr[:,iscell])\n   451→    T = fluorescence[0].shape[0]\n   452→    # The rate stored on the series is the scanner rate: with two planes interleaved it is twice\n   453→    # the rate at which any one plane is sampled, which is why dff takes n_planes as well.\n   454→    frame_rate = plane_data.rate\n   455→    neural_time_bin_size = nplanes/frame_rate*1000 # ms\n   456→\n   457→    behavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series\n   458→    \n   459→    timestamps = behavior_data['trial number'].timestamps[:] \n   460→    if T != len(timestamps):\n   461→        print(f\"Warning: number of neural time bins ({T}) does not match number of behavior timestamps ({len(timestamps)}), cropping to the minimum of the two\")\n   462→        T = min(T, len(timestamps))\n   463→        fluorescence = [f[:T] for f in fluorescence]\n   464→        neuropil = [f[:T] for f in neuropil]\n   465→        timestamps = timestamps[:T]\n   466→    \n   467→    behavior_time_bin_size = np.median(np.diff(timestamps))*1000 # ms\n   468→    \n   469→    # trial number for each time point\n   470→    trial_start_idx, trial_end_idx = get_trial_starts_ends(behavior_data['trial_start'].data[:], behavior_data['teleport'].data[:])\n   471→    ntrials = len(trial_start_idx)\n   472→    \n   473→    # reward_amounts are all the same\n   474→    reward_amounts = behavior_data['Reward'].data[:][:T]\n   475→    assert np.allclose(reward_amounts,reward_amounts[0]), f\"Reward amounts are not all the same, unique values: {np.unique(reward_amounts)}\"\n   476→    reward_times = behavior_data['Reward'].timestamps[:] # in seconds        \n   477→    # find the closest timestamp to reward times, not careful for mismatches right now\n   478→    reward_time_indices = np.searchsorted(timestamps, reward_times)\n   479→    err = timestamps[reward_time_indices] - reward_times\n   480→    assert np.all(np.abs(err) <= time_bin_size/2), f\"Reward times do not align with neural time bins, max error: {np.max(np.abs(err))} s\"\n   481→    isreward = np.zeros(T)\n   482→    isreward[reward_time_indices] = 1\n   483→\n   484→    # 14, and mice m15-m19 on day 1 and all switch days\" -- and names m17 and m18 as the two\n   524→    # animals imaged in two planes, which are the two-plane sessions in this dataset. The one cost is that on\n   537→        # keep_teleports sessions the baseline window opens two samples after the teleport onset\n   538→        # rather than their one.\n   539→        dff_curr,        f_neu=neuropil_curr.T.astype(np.float64),\n   542→                                    neuropil_method='subtract', baseline_method='maximin',\n   543→                                    subtract_baseline=True, neu_coef=0.7, tau=0.7,\n   544→                                    frame_rate=frame_rate, n_planes=nplanes, deconvolve=True,\n   545→                                    keep_teleports=keep_teleports)\n   546→        # Drop the putative interneurons the paper excludes from further analysis.\n   547→        isint = is_putative_interneuron(dff_curr, speed)\n   548→        nint += int(np.sum(isint))\n   549→        spike_data.append(events_curr[~isint].T)\n   550→    spike_data = np.concatenate(spike_data, axis=1)\n   551→    print(f\"Excluded {nint} putative interneurons of {nint + spike_data.shape[1]} cells \"\n   552→          f\"({100*nint/(nint + spike_data.shape[1]):.2f}%)\")\n   553→    # Samples between laps are NaN by construction (that is what min_distance_to_reward_zone)\n   614→        max_distance_to_reward_zone = max(distance_to_reward_zone.max(), max_distance_to_reward_zone)\n   615→\n   616→        # histogram distance to reward zone     output_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n   621→        \n   622→        # speed\n   623→        output_curr[2] = np.digitize(speed_curr, speed_bins) - 1\n   624→        \n   625→        # lick\n   626→        output_curr[3] = (licks_curr > 0).astype(int)\n   627→        \n   628→        # reward location\n   629→        output_curr[4] = ['A','B','C'].index(reward_zone_curr)\n   630→        \n   631→        # reward outcome\n   632→        output_curr[5] = np.any(isreward[idx]).astype(int)\n   633→        \n   634→        output.append(output_curr)\n   635→        \n   636→    if show_processing:\n   637→        \n   638→        ntrials = len(neural)\n   639→        trials_plot = np.linspace(0, ntrials-1, min(ntrials, distance_to_reward_zone = compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr)\n   671→            ax[2,triali].plot(timestamps_curr,distance_to_reward_zone,'.-',label='raw') # distance to reward zone\n   672→            ax[2,triali].plot(timestamps_curr,distance_bin_centers[output_curr[0]],'-',label='thresholded') # thresholded distance to reward zone\n   673→
